Many species exhibit traits that are intermediate between these extremes, or vary depending on conditions.

Human Population Growth:

The human population has grown exponentially (J-shaped curve) for centuries, especially since the Industrial Revolution, due to advances in agriculture, medicine, and sanitation that reduced death rates.

Currently over 8 billion people.

While the global growth rate has slowed, the population is still increasing significantly.

Demographic transition: A shift from high birth and death rates to low birth and death rates, often associated with industrialization and improved living standards.

Age structure diagrams (population pyramids) can predict future population growth trends.

Consequences of human population growth: Increased demand for resources (food, water, energy), habitat loss, pollution, climate change.

Estimates of Earth's carrying capacity for humans vary widely and depend on assumptions about resource consumption, technology, and environmental impact.

(Suggested insertion: A graph of human population growth over history. Examples of age structure diagrams for rapidly growing, slow-growing, and stable/declining populations.)

21.2 Community Interactions

A biological community is an assemblage of populations of different species living close enough together for potential interaction. Interactions between species (interspecific interactions) can be positive (+), negative (-), or neutral (0) for the individuals involved.

Types of Interspecific Interactions:

Competition (-/-):

Occurs when individuals of different species compete for the same limited resources (e.g., food, water, space, light). Both species are negatively affected.

Competitive Exclusion Principle (Gause's Principle): Two species competing for the same limiting resources cannot coexist permanently in the same place if their niches are identical. One species will eventually outcompete and eliminate the other.

Ecological Niche: The sum of a species' use of biotic and abiotic resources in its environment; its "ecological role."

Fundamental niche: The niche potentially occupied by a species.

Realized niche: The portion of the fundamental niche actually occupied by a species, often restricted by competition.

Resource Partitioning: Differentiation of niches that enables similar species to coexist in a community (e.g., by using different resources, or the same resources at different times or in different ways). This can lead to character displacement (evolution of divergent traits in sympatric populations of competing species).

Predation (+/-):

One species (the predator) kills and eats another species (the prey).

Drives the evolution of adaptations in both predators (e.g., claws, fangs, venom, speed, camouflage) and prey (e.g., camouflage/cryptic coloration, aposematic/warning coloration, Batesian mimicry, Müllerian mimicry, mechanical/chemical defenses, herding, mobbing).

Predator-prey interactions can lead to population cycles.

Herbivory (+/-):

An organism (herbivore) eats parts of a plant or alga.

Plants have evolved defenses against herbivores (e.g., thorns, spines, chemical toxins, tough tissues). Herbivores have coevolved adaptations to overcome these defenses.

Parasitism (+/-):

One organism (the parasite) derives its nourishment from another organism (the host), which is harmed in the process.

Endoparasites: Live within the host's body (e.g., tapeworms, malaria parasites).

Ectoparasites: Feed on the external surface of a host (e.g., ticks, lice, fleas).

Many parasites have complex life cycles involving multiple hosts.

Parasites can significantly influence host population dynamics and community structure.

Mutualism (+/+):

Both interacting species benefit.

Examples:

Pollination: Insects or other animals transfer pollen for plants in exchange for nectar or pollen.

Mycorrhizae: Fungi and plant roots (fungus gets carbohydrates, plant gets enhanced mineral absorption).

Nitrogen-fixing bacteria and legumes.

Coral animals and symbiotic algae (zooxanthellae).

Acacia trees and ants.

Can be obligate (one species cannot survive without the other) or facultative (both can survive alone).

Commensalism (+/0):

One species benefits, and the other is not significantly affected (neither harmed nor helped).

Hard to document in nature, as any close association likely has some effect.

Examples: Cattle egrets feeding on insects flushed out by grazing cattle; barnacles attaching to whales.

(Suggested insertion: For each type of interaction, provide a clear definition and iconic examples, perhaps with simple diagrams illustrating the interaction.)

Community Structure and Diversity:

Species Diversity: The variety of different kinds of organisms that make up a community. Two components:

Species Richness: The total number of different species in the community.

Relative Abundance (Species Evenness): The proportion each species represents of the total individuals in the community.

Communities with higher species diversity are generally more productive, stable, and resilient to disturbances.

Trophic Structure: The feeding relationships between organisms (food chains and food webs). (Covered in Ch 19/20).

Dominant Species: Species in a community that are the most abundant or have the highest biomass. They exert a strong influence on community structure.

Keystone Species: Not necessarily abundant, but exert strong control on community structure by their pivotal ecological roles or niches. Their removal can cause dramatic changes in the community.

Example: Sea otters (predators of sea urchins; without otters, urchins overgraze kelp forests). Beavers (ecosystem engineers that create wetlands).

Ecosystem Engineers (Foundation Species): Species that create or dramatically alter their physical environment (e.g., corals building reefs, beavers building dams, trees forming forests).

21.3 Ecological Succession

Ecological succession is the gradual process of change in species composition and community structure in a particular area over time, often following a disturbance.

Disturbance: An event (e.g., fire, flood, storm, volcanic eruption, human activities like logging or farming) that changes a community, removes organisms from it, and alters resource availability. The frequency and intensity of disturbances shape community structure.

Intermediate Disturbance Hypothesis: Suggests that moderate levels of disturbance can foster greater species diversity than either high or low levels of disturbance.

Types of Ecological Succession:

Primary Succession:

Occurs in an area where there were originally no organisms and where soil has not yet formed (e.g., on a new volcanic island, on rubble left by a retreating glacier, on bare rock).

Pioneer species (e.g., lichens, mosses) are typically the first to colonize. They help break down rock and form soil.

Gradually, other species like grasses, shrubs, and trees colonize as the soil develops and environmental conditions change.

Can take hundreds or thousands of years.

Secondary Succession:

Occurs in an area where an existing community has been cleared by a disturbance that leaves the soil intact (e.g., after a forest fire, abandoned agricultural field).

The area already has soil and often a seed bank and surviving roots/rhizomes.

Proceeds much faster than primary succession. Early colonizers are often fast-growing herbaceous plants, followed by shrubs and then trees.

(Suggested insertion: Diagrams illustrating primary succession (e.g., on bare rock) and secondary succession (e.g., after a forest fire), showing changes in vegetation over time.)

Mechanisms of Succession:

Facilitation: Early-arriving species may modify the environment in ways that make it more favorable for later-arriving species (e.g., nitrogen-fixing plants enriching soil).

Inhibition: Early species may inhibit the establishment of later species (e.g., by shading, allelopathy - releasing toxins).

Tolerance: Later species may be unaffected by early species and colonize simply because they are more tolerant of the changing conditions or are better competitors for resources.

Succession generally proceeds until a relatively stable, self-perpetuating community, often called a climax community, is reached. However, many communities are routinely affected by disturbances and may never reach a true climax state; they exist as a mosaic of patches at different successional stages.

Human activities are a major source of disturbance globally, significantly altering patterns of ecological succession and community structure. Understanding these dynamics is crucial for ecological restoration and conservation.
